ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U1	Hi! Happy men's day! Question time begins. We're gonna do a presence check. 40 members of the Riigikogu registered to be present, 61 missing. Prime Minister Taavi Rõivas, Andres Anvelt, Minister of Justice, and Urmas Kruuse, Minister of Health and Labour will participate in the Question Hour. Last day, six members of the Riigikogu expressed their wish to ask questions. I will give you two additional questions from the floor for each issue.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U2	The first questioner is Marko Mihkelson, and the question is for Prime Minister Taavi Rõivas. Please, Marko Mihkelson!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U3	Thank you, Mr President of the Riigikogu. Prime Minister! From the perspective of Estonia's future, it is undoubtedly extremely important to contribute every day to strengthening our security and also economic environment. It also concerns international cooperation and action to ensure that all agreements that we conclude with our good partners are very clearly based on those interests that will consolidate and strengthen our security in the future and create additional wealth for our country. A lot of questions have been raised in recent days by the so-called gas agreements that you signed on Monday. As with all international agreements, this mandate, which a member of the government receives, is extremely important here. What specific government decision do you grant when you conclude these agreements?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U4	Thank you. Indeed, there have been negotiations for three years, and now for the first time we have a tangible solution to how to strengthen Estonia's gas security. I am pleased that we have reached this agreement. This is not technically a contract, much less an external agreement. This is a communiqué or, let's say, a protocol of common intentions, it can be called an agreement. This agreement is based on the mandate already requested by the previous Minister for Economic Affairs from the European Commission. For the first time, if I remember correctly, it was on 1 March 2013. Then the main directions were set. At the government meeting, we last agreed exactly on the positions that we presented to Finland two weeks ago. I am pleased to say that, just as the Estonian Government confirmed these positions two weeks ago on the proposal of the Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, we reached this agreement literally. So the first mandate comes from the Riigikogu. The Riigikogu Committee on European Union Affairs gave this first foundation and then the Minister of Economic Affairs was Juhan Parts.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U5	Marko Mihkelson, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U6	Thank you. As we know, this is a very important long-term agreement that affects both the security of supply of our gas supply and whether or not investment in Estonia comes directly. Estonia has made it very clear throughout this process that this investment is just a regional LNG terminal that should come to such a place that our consumers would also be guaranteed a better price, but at the same time this investment would be more efficient. At the end of the summer, the representatives of the European Commission were clearly of the opinion that this project, prepared in Estonia, has a clear head start compared to the project of Finnish colleagues. When you conclude this agreement, what analysis do you consider that the regional terminal to be built in Finland is better than the terminal to be built in Estonia?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U7	Thank you. Reaching an agreement is much more preferable from the point of view of Estonia than a situation where we do not have an agreement. The European Commission has made the co-financing of both the connections and the regional terminal conditional on Estonia and Finland reaching an agreement. As long as we did not have an agreement, we had no confidence that we could take a real step forward in this area, so it was extremely important to reach an agreement. This agreement, or the steps we take, can be assessed from two points of view. In my opinion, security must be a priority in the case of gas supply. Of course, I do not deny the economic aspect. But if we have to choose between two solutions, one of which is as profitable as possible for Estonian companies, and the other guarantees us that all those people whose homes are heated by gas are heated, whatever happens, then the primary interest of the Estonian state is precisely to ensure security, so that the rooms are warm in every situation. Therefore, for quite a long time, this was also the first preference of the previous government to establish connections between Finland and Estonia, as well as to establish them between Lithuania and Poland. This decision is based not only on the analysis of our own country, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications and Elering, for example, but also on an impartial study, which clearly confirms that the impact of the Community on Estonia's energy security is the most important.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U8	The second point, Marko Mihkelson, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U9	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Prime Minister! As you know, some Estonian energy experts have criticised this agreement precisely from the point of view you have emphasised. It is claimed that the proposed gas pipeline between the two Gazprom systems does not necessarily increase security. For example, Professor Arvi Hamburg of the Chair of Energy Systems at Tallinn University of Technology has also said that this pipe would rather reduce our energy security and, at the same time, increase the price of possible network services for the gas consumer, because the maintenance costs of both Balticconnector and SOS terminal will be added to the network service fee. Thus, it is, in essence, only costly for the Estonian state, not useful.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U10	Thank you. Not some experts, but just the Arvi Hamburg you mentioned has really criticised it, apart from yourself, of course. I think that many members of the Riigikogu still remember what Arvi Hamburg's views were when the natural gas law was discussed. At that time he spoke about Eesti Gaas, partly owned by Gazprom. He spoke a story that in no way overlapd with the interests of Estonia's security. I do not want to suspect him personally or even less blame him, but if we look rationally, we will see that his story does not actually coincide with the interests of Estonian security. The fact that the construction of the gas pipeline would somehow be too much for the Estonian consumer is not true. Currently, the gas core network has a tariff of EUR 50 million and slightly more. The construction of a gas pipeline means that the amount of the tariff to be considered will increase to about one-and-a-half times, with an impact on the gas bill of 1.5%. This effect exists, but if on one side there is 1.5% of the price of gas and on the other side there is confidence that Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania cannot be cut off from the gas network and we can get gas from Finland if necessary, then I say that confidence is worth more. All the more so, in the case of the construction of a terminal, we will not talk about the additional percentages, but then the gas tariff will be several times higher.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U11	Imre Sooäär, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U12	Thank you, good manager. Prime Minister! Energy security is undoubtedly an issue where every country in the European Union has its own interests. Of course, these are the interests of the countries. However, in order to move forward at all, these countries must make some compromises between themselves. In these matters, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications has taken long steps forward in the last eight months. Personally, as a former minister, I would not be jealous of this either, but rather feel that it is good that a number of important things have moved forward. As Prime Minister, I thank and congratulate the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, headed by Urve Palo, not only for this gas agreement, but also for a number of other things. The Rail Baltic agreement is also international and, in the meantime, it seemed that it would not come at all, but here, too, a few months ago, they made a breakthrough. My role in this agreement, I'd say, is highly overrated. Yes, of course, it is important that the Prime Ministers pressured both sides in a good way so that the negotiations would end effectively and we would not be in a stalemate again, which would be very bad for Estonia, because that would mean that we would not have an alternative to Russian gas. Now this alternative arises. So, of course, the Prime Ministers had a role to play, but the main work was still done by the officials in both countries, as well as by the companies that participated in the negotiations. The recognition that an agreement has been reached naturally belongs to them too.

I am proud of the Estonian Defence Forces. I'm proud of the Ämari. I believe that the political debate of political parties must certainly address the issue of security. If you look closely at this clip, you will understand that my message there is this: we must make an effort to be even better protected. This is what I intend to continue to stand for, both as Prime Minister and as Chairman of a political party.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U18	The next questioner is Kadri Simson and the question is the Minister of Health and Labour, Urmas Kruus. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U19	Thank you. Unfortunately, Juhan Parts has now left the hall; he could have asked Urmas Kruus for an additional question. But my question concerns the following matter. Last January, the health partners signed a cooperation agreement on goodwill. Its aim was to put in place policies and concrete actions to ensure the sustainability of the health care system. The Auditor General was here last Wednesday. In the recent report of the National Audit Office, we can read that the Ministry of Social Affairs is unable to comply with the goodwill agreement with health workers. Yesterday, you signed a similar goodwill agreement to ensure the social protection of people with disabilities or health impairment. What should give us reason to believe that the agreement concluded by the Ministry of Social Affairs will comply with this?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U20	Thank you, Reverend Asker! I think you interpreted the words of the Auditor General a little one-sided. I can also explain this on the basis of so-called round table agreements. The Health Round Table Agreement was one of the reasons why we could enter into a similar agreement in social welfare. It was only this autumn that the Ministry of Social Affairs presented to the government office 'Estonian Health Developments until 2020', which addresses the common part agreed between the parties at these round tables as developments. One of the most important changes has taken place in primary health care. When we look at the audit of the Auditor General, we must admit that he has referred to this change as a very positive trend. The role of primary care, family health centres, family doctors and their new crew members in health care is a clear upward trend. It's very important. The second part of the audit relates to the availability of services. In order to get the services even better, a very large-scale eHealth project has been launched, with a number of important changes foreseen, be they related to e-mails, e-consultation or launch of digital registrations. For this reason, I think that we clearly agree with the Auditor General on these directions and that we want to move forward with this round table agreement, because there is still a lot of important things to discuss and the discussions have reached a very interesting stage.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U21	Kadri Simson, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U22	Thank you. Minister, thank you very much. I did not quote the National Audit Office in a biased way. Point 155 of the agreement states: 'The agreement sets out a number of activities (with deadlines) which should make the health care system more efficient and improve the situation of the parties. However, only a few points have been fulfilled so far from the agreement... " Very many organisations of the disabled are reluctant to sign a goodwill agreement. They did not do so yesterday, as the work capacity reform agenda as it stands does not improve the chances of these people entering the labour market. What is the value of the goodwill agreement, and to whom is it intended if even the representatives of the disabled are not prepared to sign it?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U23	Thank you, Reverend Asker! In the Riigikogu and in Estonia at all, targets are set, and from time to time we must admit that not always the agreements that have been hoped for at the beginning will be reached by the deadline. But through these agreements, we have shown very clearly the directions and actions that we have been able to carry out in the light of either the economic situation or the common part. If we are talking about this goodwill agreement, which is partly linked to the work capacity reform, most of all to the part that simply does not fit into the two draft laws, the two drafts cannot solve all the problems of social welfare in Estonia as a whole, then its value is related to the fact that as a collective part we recognise the objectives that we should pursue in order to improve the living environment of people with reduced working capacity, people with disabilities and their ability to cope with them in the Republic of Estonia. I am convinced that we will not be helped by confrontation, but if we have good will and we want to do something together. Yesterday the representatives of the various institutions were at the table, and there were also representatives of disabled people's organisations. I cannot agree in any way that their representative organisations are excluded from this agreement.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U24	Kadri Simson, second question, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U25	Thank you. The press release of the Ministry of Social Affairs did not mention the organisations that signed the agreement, but I asked for an extra list and saw that most of the signatories were not directly related to disabled people. But last week, you admitted that postponing the work capacity reform will not deprive Estonia of euro money. In this regard, Postimees published an article entitled 'Minister Kruuse's vass with money numbers'. It turned out that we would miss the money if we didn't do the work capacity reform. By 2018, Estonia has promised that 31420 people with special needs will receive help from the unemployment fund in finding a job. This means that the person has been consulted, trained or otherwise assisted. That doesn't mean he's got a job. But what happens to the rest of the disabled when there is a commitment that only 31000 people will be offered services in the next three years?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U26	Thank you, Reverend Asker! What you have referred to means, in my opinion, that I really have said with clear knowledge that if we do not implement the labour capacity reform, we will certainly have no basis for using European Union money in the way we have promised the European Commission. The negotiations were very long. Estonian officials worked very hard to bring this money to Estonia. Now we have it. Another important change in the use of European Union money is that it is very important for the European Commission that Member States use intermediate indicators to show whether they are moving in the direction that has been considered important. As a result, not all money will be issued, but 94% of these funds will first be released, and if the interim indicators are not met, there may be a risk that the remaining amount will no longer be distributed. Specifically, for these two draft laws, this could mean almost EUR 14 million, but if we look at the work programme more broadly or at the measures that are related to employment, it will certainly be higher. That is why we must be very active in ensuring that these people receive the services and that we also meet the milestones. This reform is not designed simply to meet the indicators, but to change the current situation, with which no one is satisfied, and clearly for the better.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U27	Margus Tsahkna, please!

Take, for example, the availability of rehabilitation or aids. The order of two years is not reasonable. Now there has been a change that the order can last at most one year.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U30	Lembit Rockvee, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U31	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Good minister! In life, reforms irritate someone and make one, two, and three afraid. If we look at the target group.. we are talking about people with reduced working capacity, which are nearly 100,000 and half of whom are people with disabilities, then people with disabilities have, in my opinion, been positive. They have been very active actors and speakers because they have sensed that their voice has been allowed to sound in society and that it sounds farther and harder than maybe in the last ten years. I fully understand that emotional conflicts can also arise. Of course, we can say that the approaching elections can also fuel certain emotions. But above all, I see it as very important that within the same five or six months, when there has been a very active debate in society, the attitude has already begun to change. We have heard that there is now a queue behind the doors of the Astangu Rehabilitation Centre, because employers want to receive training and training on how to deal better with people with reduced working capacity to provide them with work later. This, too, is a very important sign of the change that we have been able to bring about in a short time.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U33	I will conclude on this issue.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U34	The next questioner is Jüri Morozov. The question is for Minister of Justice Andres Anvelt. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U35	Thank you. Dear Minister, My question is about the review of criminal proceedings. I know that you are quite critical of the existing procedure. You have said that our procedure is a crooked house with a Soviet foundation, German walls and the roof of an American competing trial. What are the Ministry's further plans in the area of criminal proceedings? As a minister, how do you imagine the future of criminal proceedings, and what problems do you see there?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U36	Thank you, good questioner! There were several parts of your question, but I will start from the beginning. First of all, the current criminal procedure applies to us since 2004. This was a major step forward from the criminal proceedings so far, indeed during the occupation, which were based on the inquisition method. In 2004, we lost very much from the old system, but we kept a number of fundamental principles the same, really bringing in elements of German law and an American competing element in the trial. Given the resources we have for the procedure, criminal proceedings certainly need to be reviewed from the point of view of making it quicker, reducing duplication and modernising it. What does that mean? Faster, as well as modernisation, requires the introduction of completely new methods of formalising evidence. For example, better digital proofs, statutory fixation, the introduction of a digital file, from the police or the tax and customs officer to the court. There is certainly a need to review the investigative element both in the pre-trial investigation and in a court where, unfortunately, there is often duplication at the moment, in other words, the same things that are done in the pre-trial investigation will be re-established in a court of law. All of this often disturbs citizens precisely because, whatever their role, they must waste a great deal of their time in order to comply with the bureaucratic requirements of legal proceedings.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U37	Jüri Morozov, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U38	Thank you. Dear Minister, At the beginning of the last century, Anton Hansen also wrote about the revision of Tammsaare. He said: " Reviding is as often needed as bread, water, and air. After all, the subject specifically concerns people. You've said there's a complete vacuum in this system and there's air to be provided. What measures are envisaged as a result of this review to bring the system into the air?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U39	Thank you. Let's say that the complex of measures is large. The review of criminal proceedings is only one part of it and will be included in the Ministry's action plan for next year. Certainly, the review of criminal proceedings cannot be a very fast process, as criminal proceedings are one of the most damaging forms of procedure for citizens' fundamental rights and freedoms. Therefore, any change should be considered with all experts on the basis of both theory and practice. But internal security, let's say it's the procedural side that requires a lot of air in the future. Currently, the Ministry of Justice has been subject to judicial reform for several years. When dealing with judges in courthouses, it is understood that judicial proceedings are becoming faster, for example, in administrative or civil proceedings. In criminal proceedings, the fact that proceedings come from the prosecutor's office, especially complicated procedures, is often an aggravating factor. There are a number of things to be considered here. Firstly, to ensure that the number of practitioners is sufficient and, secondly, to prevent them from doing empty work. Empty work is not about solving crimes, it is about repeating and repeating what has already been done once when the evidence is fixed. I also attach great importance to keeping these professionals in work and continuing training. All of this requires resources both in the prosecutor's office, where we begin preparations for structural changes, and certainly also in the Police and Border Guard Board, which is under the administration of the Ministry of Internal Affairs.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U40	Jüri Morozov, the second point, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U41	Thank you. Dear Minister, In answering the first question, you spoke about the need to digitise the procedure. Can you tell us how much the Estonian state's money will be spent on digitisation and how much it could actually affect the speed of the process, the reduction in labour needs, etc.?
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U42	Thank you. How much money will be spent on the whole process of proceedings, from the judge to the Police and Border Guard Board, the Tax and Customs Board, or other authorities with procedural rights, to the court to the court, this is all the same issue of analysis. We are still preparing the so-called digital file project at the trial stage. From this point of view, we will assess how much money is needed to expand this project, so that it all reaches the prosecutor's office and the police (let's be summarised as one or the other, no matter where the same function is performed). That's what the same review of criminal proceedings shows. The introduction of the digital file has several objectives. One objective is to facilitate the work of the parties to proceedings so that it is not necessary to physically transfer documents by bone between different stages of the proceedings and all the evidence and other material necessary for the proceedings are available in one place to all parties to the proceedings who, at some point, have the right of access to one or the other. The second objective is certainly that citizens who are involved in criminal proceedings can easily know, for example, at what stage one or the other procedure is. In this way we can reduce the current workload. For example, in order to send notifications to all victims that the procedure has been closed for one reason or another, prosecutors will need 30,000 hours a year. So there's something to look at and make more modern.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U45	Thank you. First of all, I am pleased that not only members of the Socialist Group are interested in this issue. If I remember correctly, they can save nearly 14,000 processing hours. We have to get to the point where if the judge, prosecutor or judge has done something once, he shouldn't have to do it again.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U50	The next questioner is Peter Laurson. The question is the Minister of Health and Labour, Urmas Kruus. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U51	Thank you, good manager. Dear Minister for Health and Labour, At meetings with the people of the city of Tartu, I have been asked several times what you have achieved as a former Head of Government as Minister of Health and Labour. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U52	Thank you. This means that if we are now used to being served by a family doctor with only a sister on his team and who will direct us to a specialist, then in the future there will be a midwife, a physical therapist and a nurse on the family doctor's team. All of this is based on this principle, so that we can do more preventive work and detect diseases at an early stage so that they can be treated quickly, and we can influence people's health behaviour in a positive direction. It is particularly important to change people's behaviour in those areas which the WHO has also pointed out and on which it has reproached us: men's health, smoking, alcohol policy. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U87	We will start working on the points on today's agenda. The first item is the third reading of draft law 677 on child protection initiated by the Government of the Republic. I'm opening negotiations. Mailis Reps, please, on behalf of the Central Party Group.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U88	Eight minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U89	I'm asking for more time! Wait, please, Mailis, let them calm down!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U90	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! We have come to the round of speeches prior to the final vote on the Child Protection Act. I will address some of the questions raised by the Centre Party Group. We are in a situation where, unfortunately, there are many children living next to us, whose daily coping is difficult and who need side-by-side intervention and help, whether it be because of poverty or loss of parental care. In order to improve the situation of children, more cooperation between the state and local government is essential, even if only to ensure that there is a child welfare worker in every local government. Currently, only 38% of municipalities employ a special specialist for child welfare work. In the rest of the municipalities, social workers work in child protection, who have many tasks other than child protection, and therefore the detection of children in distress may be affected and intervention may be delayed. The Group of the Estonian Centre Party naturally considers it important to complete the Child Protection Act in line with the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child, which gives priority to the interests of the child. Unfortunately, however, the new draft law on child protection has raised a great many questions in society, and today, when we are in the final phase of the debate, a number of problems remain unresolved. First of all, there has been concern in society, which is perhaps the most important question, that poverty may be the reason why a child is separated from the family. Why is such a concern in society? The reason is the fear that we will follow the example of Scandinavia. Social media is full of examples of cases in Sweden and Norway, also in Finland, where thousands of children are separated from the family each year, and very often because of the family's financial problems alone. We therefore need to give certainty that we are talking in the new law more about aid measures where the state and local government have to intervene, and poverty must not and cannot be the reason for family separation. In addition, during the procedure of the draft law on child protection, child protection workers have identified a number of problems that arise in their daily work, which this draft law does not solve. I'll name three of them. First of all, the most important one. Child welfare workers are very concerned that they are not getting information on a child in danger quickly enough. Family doctors, not even schools or other institutions of the child, such as recreational schools, who are constantly in contact with the child and could spot and perhaps spot problems with the child, do not have an obligation to be informed. Second, access to the child. Even if children's guards receive information about a child in danger, the child's family still lacks the obligation to cooperate with child protection, this obligation does not come out of this law. Children's guards are worried. The problem of opening the door alone has been discussed quite a bit in society. It may be that the situation of a child in danger is discussed by the media, especially social media, and in public spaces, basically all decisions have been made about the family, but there are very few opportunities for childguards to actually intervene or assist the child. As a result, children's guards proposed to the Commission which, unfortunately, either due to lack of time or unwillingness, was essentially undisputed. What opportunities for influence have they offered? They have requested that child benefits, in particular parental benefits, be linked to cooperation with child protection and custody institutions. Life has shown that very often child benefits are increasing right now, but especially parental benefits, this means that ultimately this family, who has not been very cooperative, will start to cooperate quickly quite surprisingly. These are extreme cases. Fortunately, there are not many of them in society, but precisely on the basis of these very sad cases and in order to try to prevent them from happening in the future, we are dealing with this bill. It is also important to note the introduction of an administrative procedure, which I also pointed out at second reading on behalf of the Central Party Group and myself. Children's guards have been anxious about 48 hours in the original draft and 72 hours later, during which time they will have to get a decision from the court as to whether or not a child needs to be separated from the family. The reaction of childguards has been unambiguous: there must certainly not be a situation where, either due to procedural procedures or a lack of court response rate, the child must be returned to the family, regardless of the extreme need, before a solution is found. These are just a few of the problems that have been highlighted during the procedure for this draft. But in conclusion, it may be appropriate to pay attention to what has caused fear. The whole approach to child protection and the rights of the child raises questions. In the light of this law, which is likely to be adopted today, society is being given a kind of signal that children are somehow separate from the family. As the Commission rapporteur told us at second reading, the concept of family was introduced into the law declaratively. This is the message that has been most painfully commented on, this aspect must not be underestimated. Children, especially young children, need family support; they also, of course, need personal protection, but the whole concept cannot be built on the fact that children need protection from their parents. However, we are talking about the child's right to be with his family. The family must receive support for a very long time before extreme measures are needed. In summing up, it can be said that, as there are very many issues in the air, the Central Party Group does not support the adoption of this draft as a law. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U91	Heljo Pikhof, please, on behalf of the Group of the Social Democratic Party. Eight minutes in total.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U92	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Good fellows! The closer the elections, the more there will be situations where the draft, which has been fully approved at first reading, is no longer fit for second reading. So now it's the Child Protection Act. We are being painted with a colorful picture of an evil official pulling a crying child out of a loving home with power. However, if there is a crying child in the picture, there is no need for deepening or argument; emotions are still on the ceiling. Let's go back in time. Act number one: 18 June and the elections are over six months. At the end of the first reading, Margus Tsahkna thanks both the draft authors and ministers for their good work on behalf of the Fatherland and Res Publica Union. It mentions the new Child Protection Act as a reform and welcomes the fact that the draft has been discussed very thoroughly with the parties and that the implementation part has also been agreed with them. I quote Margus Tsahkna: 'You certainly think I come here with a bad and evil message. But vice versa. I think that the draft law on child protection that we have before us today could be a good example of how the draft law should be drawn up. It's done a lot of work. This work has been very methodological and has involved a large number of people. ' Then he highlights the good aspects of the bill and draws attention to some minor problems, while expressing hope: 'But these are small things that, I really hope, will be dealt with after the adoption of the Child Protection Act when this law is implemented. ' Margus Tsahkna adds: 'I think we can only accept this law in the Chamber of the Riigikogu. If we are going to process the state budgets for the coming years, we should remember that we have adopted such a good law, and make sure that its implementation is financially guaranteed. This could be a cross-party issue. I don't think there's gonna be a political caption here. I wish everyone strength and courage in implementing this law. IRL supports this law. ' I must say that Margus Tsahkna, as the only member of the IRL, tabled an amendment to the draft law on child protection. This concerned the temporary removal of a child in danger from the family. This amendment was also taken into account by the Commission. Act two: November 5th. Almost five months have passed since the first reading and the elections are at the door. The draft is at second reading in the Chamber. Many IRL members are extremely critical of the draft and express their views on the platform. I quote Priit Sibulat: 'First, the bill creates an understanding that children should be protected from their parents. A vision of a conflicting relationship between children and parents will be created, and then a regulation will be developed to ensure the help of the state to the weaker party, the child. The bill carries a socialist mentality, according to which the state is increasingly interfering with the law of parents to raise and care for their children. Parents will be reduced to a position where their natural right to raise their children and care for them will be treated as a privilege granted by the State. 'Jne, et cetera. Kaia Iva summarises on behalf of the group: 'The draft is a long step in the right direction, but there are no more general provisions on child protection in the draft. Children whose life or health is not in danger are out of observation. As a fundamental principle of child welfare, the right to grow in a family has not been laid down. In order to remedy these shortcomings, we propose that the second reading of the draft be discontinued. Since the aim is to enforce the law from 2016, there is no time pressure, and it would probably be possible to provide a large part of the explanations in the draft explanatory memorandum. This would be the fastest and most effective way to prevent any further delay, as it would be regrettable if the interpretations of the law were to become apparent through painful, time-consuming and family-traumatic case law. ' It should be noted that for the second reading, the Commission supplemented the draft with such a sentence: 'The natural environment for the development and growth of a child is family. ' The Centre Party has tabled four amendments to the draft. On behalf of their group, too, the second reading highlighted points that should have been regulated differently, but unfortunately we have only heard most of them in the Chamber. For example, a number of paragraphs cannot be applied without further interpretation or explanation. The question arises as to what these paragraphs are. It was argued that child protection workers are very concerned about the emphasis on § 1 in relation to the administrative procedure law, etc. For this 72 hours, which has been very much here, I can say that the Centre Party has tabled an amendment that provided for 96 hours here, and I supported it in the Commission. Now I'm talking about the law. After the entry into force of the Child Protection Act, the protection of family and private life will not be reduced, but, on the contrary, will increase. In what way? In particular, the separation of a child from the family is governed by the family law currently in force since July 2010. Our opponents are happy to overlook this fact. Under both this law and the future Child Protection Act, a child can only be separated from the family if the child's life or health is in danger if the parent has caused the danger and refuses to provide assistance to the child. The Child Welfare Act does not regulate the separation of a child from the family; it is governed by the Family Act, which entered into force in July 2010. § 134 states that the separation of a child from the family can be based on the abuse of custody by a parent. Family law states that abuse of custody is the neglect of a child, the inability of parents to perform their duties or the conduct of a third party, and that parents do not want or are unable to prevent a danger and that it threatens the physical, mental or mental well-being of the child. The risk to the child's well-being is assessed by the court in the collection and whether the local government has taken other measures to support the child and the family. So we already have everything regulated by law. Thus, a mere economic livelihood of the family, a small income or a material deprivation cannot be a separate reason for separating the child from the family. It must be demonstrated to the Court that the parent is a threat to the child's well-being. Now I'm gonna talk a little bit more about the law. The current Child Protection Act, which dates back to 1993, consists mainly of slogans, while not organising or properly concluding the role of the institutions concerned. In order to protect children as best as possible, the new law must be specific. At last, the duties of the government, the Ministry of Social Affairs, county governments, local governments and the Social Insurance Board have been regulated. It is very important that this draft also provides for the obligation for all persons who have information on the child's need for assistance to report to the child who are in danger and regulates the situation in which the child needs help or is in danger. The Social Democrats support this bill. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U97	Laine Randjärv, please, on behalf of the Reform Party Group. Three minutes extra time.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U98	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I am extremely pleased with what the previous speaker said about the need to listen to what grandmothers say, because they are always right about everything that concerns children. Therefore, as a grandmother, I am very pleased to present the views of the Reform Party Group. Unfortunately, the draft law on child protection pending before the Riigikogu has brought with it uneasy questions that may arise primarily from a lack of understanding of the text of the bill, but in some cases also from deliberate propaganda. I believe that, for the sake of clarity, some clarifications must be given, apart from those that have already been given by the previous speakers in this Chamber. I think repeating is the mother of wisdom. The drafting of the draft and the negotiations with associations dealing with children and families have been comprehensive, regardless of what they are trying to say. Thus, the bill has become much more meaningful than it was when it first came to this hall. In the debate on the draft law, the opponents of the draft act have put two main arguments at the forefront: the increase in the rights of the child protection worker is condemned and it is intimidated as if the new draft would allow the child protection worker to take the child from the family easily. The extension of the decision-making power of a child protection worker is certainly positive, as the extension of rights also involves strict standards of competence and training obligations. The Social Affairs Committee has also included in the draft, for example, a proposal made by the Child Protection House network, which obliges the local government to set up a child and family committee at the municipal or municipal administration as an advisory body if necessary, so that only one official does not decide on the fate of the child. With regard to the second provision, it is important to point out that a child can only be separated from the family if the child's life or health is in danger, if this danger is caused by the parent and if the parent refuses to provide assistance to the child. Only then, and no other way, can a child in danger be separated from the family that caused him trouble, and then only temporarily, for up to 72 hours. So all the fears that a child will simply be taken away from the family are completely unfounded. If you go into the existing legal provisions, you have to admit that according to the law in force, it is even easier to take a child away from the family. Children's organisations have pointed out that the child is more likely to be separated from the family in case of danger, either too late or not at all. Certainly, the child must not be taken away from the family at ease, but timely action can guarantee his safety and even his life. Once again, no one is going to take the baby away from a normal and secure environment. The draft also pays more attention to the prevention of problems with children. Estonian UNICEF has emphasised that the law must also increase the responsibility of parents and the family for the safety of children. This has been done in the course of the changes. The main objective of the Child Welfare Act is to protect the rights of all children, whether they live with their parents, guardians, carers or in a substitute home. What, then, does the new Child Welfare Act change? I'll tell you points by points, it'll be easy to understand. First of all, the law lays down the principles of child welfare and rights, which are mandatory across sectors and for all state, local government and other institutions. Among other things, cooperation, prevention and communication, the responsibility of the parent and the person raising the child, as well as the importance of the family as an environment for the development and growth of the child, are set out. Secondly, the law regulates for the first time the organisation of child protection, including the tasks of the Government of the Republic, the Ministry of Social Affairs, the County Government, the Local Government and the Social Insurance Board. Thirdly, the law imposes requirements on the child protection worker and his/her qualifications and restrictions on working with children. Fourthly, the law defines the principles governing the treatment of the child, including the pursuit of the child's interests and the prohibition of abuse. Fifthly, the law regulates a situation where a child needs help when his or her well-being is at risk, is suspected of mistreatment, neglect or something like that, or is in danger and his or her life or health is at risk. Sixthly, the law contains provisions on the protection of children separated from the family and the responsibilities of children's institutions. Seventhly, the law describes national and administrative supervision. When the Child Protection Act comes into force, the protection of family and private life will improve rather than deteriorate, as has been argued in many cases. The Reform Party supports the adoption of the draft as a law and calls on everyone to vote in favour of this draft. Thank you for your attention.

We are at a point where we no longer pay the arguments, but the belief that the reform will start in the form presented. However, as a common element, a large part of the target group, people with reduced capacity for work, senses the need for reform and the necessity for its implementation. The understanding that the reform of working capacity is indispensable is confirmed by independent studies and the views of the reform partners. All of them have been studied in the design of the reform and a large part has been taken into account. In order to involve people with special needs and reduced working capacity in society and employment, we will have a large amount of EUR 354 million for the coming seven years, both the capital of the Estonian state and the capital of the European Social Fund. This means that, with the support of the European Union, we will be able to carry out an extremely necessary and inevitable reform, for which we should find the means anyway. I would like to thank all parties, including the Ministry of Social Affairs, the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People and its sub-organisations, as well as the representatives of all other target groups, the unemployment fund and the Social Affairs Committee officials for the great work that has been done in preparing the draft legislation on labour capacity reform. In particular, I would like to express my thanks to the opposition for the critical sense and the substantive amendments, many of which have been expressed in the draft law. Ladies and gentlemen, I will once again highlight some of the essential foundations and principles on which the reform of working capacity is based. There is a significant improvement in helping people with reduced working capacity to find a job and keep a job. Focusing on each case, the principle of individual approach applies. People with a lack of working capacity will be guaranteed a higher income. A comprehensive package is offered to people with reduced working capacity. The assessment of the ability to work, the payment of the allowance and the provision of services are all carried out in one institution, the Estonian Unemployment Insurance Fund. The measures are linked and targeted. From a national perspective, income protection will be created for all people of working age and services will be provided on similar grounds. Social protection for people of working age is removed from the pension system. By the way, it has a symbolic meaning and contributes to a change of attitude. Work capacity reform relies on cooperation with and advice from employers. In addition, the draft law amending the Social Welfare Act, the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act and other laws will provide these people with important social support both in everyday life and in working life. New services will be created for people with reduced working capacity, including job protection and occupational rehabilitation. The organisation of assistance, social rehabilitation and special care services will be improved. To sum it up. Work capacity reform is destroying prejudice. This makes the opportunities for people with reduced working capacity on the labour market and for inclusion in society considerably more comprehensive and similar to those of non-essential members of society. But that doesn't mean we don't have to go any further. Great work is ahead of us, with prevention of reduced working capacity and the establishment of a system of insurance for accidents at work and occupational diseases, so that both the employer and the employee are involved in reducing and avoiding work-related accidents and occupational diseases. Ladies and gentlemen, I'm gonna finish with a little more personal approach. Last week, at second reading of the draft laws, I was asked about personal coping. I then described my livelihood here in the Riigikogu. I told you I'd use a radio aid, a transcribation service on the remote, as well as other hearing aids. Thanks to the generous support of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu, I have all these opportunities, but unfortunately many do not. Our duty, I repeat, it is our duty to guarantee the same opportunities for everyone who needs them. The Reform Party invites all of you to create these opportunities and to vote in favour of the two draft legislation on labour capacity reform today! Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U116	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! A third of the current disability pensioners are working, but two thirds are not. Around 40,000 of them would be happy to work; they would like to earn a decent salary and participate in society if only there was a chance. This is not only a matter of living standards, but also of human dignity. As labour shortages worsen, employers are also becoming more interested in employing people with reduced capacity to work. The cost of helping a person with special needs to get on the job, or the lack of skills of a potential employee, may be an obstacle. However, employers who have so far dared to hire disabled workers strongly recognise their motivation and loyalty. Work capacity reform has started to help as many people as possible to work with complex and flexible measures. All workers, employers and also the economy as a whole with reduced working capacity will win. The greatest value can be considered to be a change in the way a person thinks. Of course, this is not happening overnight, yet we are no longer talking about incapacity for work but ability. After all, women of a middle age with joint illness and people of all ages with intellectual disabilities belong to the current pensioners for incapacity for work. The construction of ramps does not help some or others, although people with reduced working capacity tend to be depicted in wheelchairs. As many people as there are, there are needs. Everyone, individually, wills, abilities and barriers will be dealt with by the unemployment fund, which helps people find suitable jobs and provides re-training, tools and other things necessary for starting work. The Unemployment Insurance Fund will also be responsible for advising and assisting employers in applying a person with reduced working capacity to work. Not all the improvements can be made, and there is no point in talking here, but I will mention some important things. A single-door policy is being pursued, which has been crucial for people with disabilities. In other words, if a person goes in through the unemployment fund's door, he or she can apply at the same time to assess his or her working capacity and determine his or her disability and to receive a disability benefit and disability benefit. If necessary, he or she will receive an aid and be directed towards receiving rehabilitation services. Transport and support services are also provided for those who are not otherwise employed. Support person service is available for 1000 hours in the first year. One door also means that a person of working age with part-time work ability receives all the services necessary to find a job or to stay at work, whether it is advice, rehabilitation at work or adjustment of the job from the unemployment fund, while the Social Insurance Board provides support for people who do not have the capacity to work. As far as rehabilitation plans are concerned, the current regime has rightly been accused of venting public money. In order to receive occupational rehabilitation, it is no longer necessary to go first to prepare a plan. The need for rehabilitation, like all other employment services, is assessed by the case manager of the trained unemployment fund. In cooperation with the client, it is agreed to receive rehabilitation services or participate in the programme. The scope and choice of health restoration services should increase considerably and, of course, people and their needs should be taken into account. People with partial working capacity who are going to receive rehabilitation services through the unemployment fund may in future receive health-enhancing, health-enhancing services for up to EUR 1 500 per year instead of the current EUR 483. They are available to people who are looking for work and to those who work. Social democrats insist that rehabilitation services are also provided for people with reduced capacity to work who do not meet the requirements for activity. The age limit for students has been lost. Learning was originally intended to be taken into account for the activity requirement only until the age of 31. The Unemployment Insurance Fund is going to pay travel support from the European Social Fund if the staff member with special needs cannot use public transport. It is important that the unemployment fund actively cooperates with local authorities and finds solutions to provide social services in cooperation with them. The European Social Fund is intended to support the development and availability of local government services. In particular, municipalities are provided with a large injection of money to support the provision of services to people with special needs. Let's not leave people with a part-time job to fight for a job on their own! I repeat what I said in the debate on behalf of the group on the previous draft law: many of our group's members convinced us to vote in favour of the two draft amendments to the labour force reform in this Chamber on 6 November. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U141	Good manager! Ladies and gentlemen, The purpose of the draft law is to help students who find it difficult to cope with their household's resources during their studies on an ad hoc basis. The need-based education allowance system is based on the student's economic needs and concerns only those students whose family's economic situation does not allow them access to higher education. Since the development of needs-based education support, we have stressed that the aim of the support is to support and encourage the acquisition of higher education for young people from families on a permanent basis with lower incomes. Applying for needs-based education allowance is currently based on the tax data of the family in the previous calendar year. However, the needs-based Special Support Fund created by the legislative amendment is intended for students whose income, according to the records, exceeded the upper limit of income at the time of applying for the need-based education allowance, but whose economic situation has unexpectedly deteriorated. A special allowance of EUR 135 can be applied for once per semester. Universities will process applications to the proposed Need-Based Special Support Fund because they have the most information and can ask for additional documents from students. It is important to mention that, like needs-based learning support, special student support is not included in income when determining income support. As a result of the legislative amendment, all full-time students who have completed at least 75% of the curriculum instead of 100% can apply for needs-based study support. We have chosen a more flexible approach because, according to studies, students face difficulties in completing the 100% curriculum that underpins the study grant. The main objective of needs-based learning support is to take account of the socio-economic situation of the student, which is why we will continue to require full-time learning, but we have decided to make the study volume requirement more flexible, reducing it from 100% to 75%. Our aim is to ensure that good students have the opportunity to obtain higher education in a way that does not hinder the economic situation. This is the objective of the draft. Thank you.

In addition to ministers, Deputy Governor Madis Müller has also met with us to discuss this topic, namely on 3 November. On 13 November we had a meeting with representatives of local government associations. Two main issues were discussed: the revenue base of local governments and the development of compensation mechanisms for the loss of special-marked fuel. On 11 November, the Committee on Monetary Affairs received an overview of the state of the national reserves. Now I will briefly talk about the amendments. By the deadline for submitting amendments, 5 November, the members of the Riigikogu and the political groups submitted a total of 23 amendments to the draft. The explanations of the amendments are set out in the list of amendments which the drafters referred to as one or the other. The Finance Committee itself drew up one proposal, which mainly provided for technical changes within the remit of several ministries, also clarified the textual paragraphs. The Commission's amendment has been drawn up on the basis of additional information from ministries and specific explanations have been included in each amendment clause in the list of amendments. Most of these changes, as I mentioned, are technical; many are involved in bringing the salaries of ministries to the right line. It is likely that the exact increase will not be clear until recent government meetings and some ministries, where these salary increases are on different budget lines, may not be able to raise the correct number of budget in the right line at the time of submission. This has been a normal practice, which was confirmed this year. One perhaps more important and substantive amendment, which was included in the Commission's amendments, is the channelling of additional funds to the national borders, which is almost EUR 2 million in total. I will talk briefly about the amendments received from the political groups and members of the Riigikogu. As I mentioned, they are available to everyone and their justifications are equally available. Unfortunately, none of these proposals were supported by the majority of the Commission. In general, the causes were in the sources of cover. The cover was provided for the reserve of the Government of the Republic. However, according to the Ministry of Finance, there are planned expenditures that make this picture slightly different from what it seems at first glance. It is intended to provide reimbursements to the European Union, to finance the IT agency through the RKAS, for example, to finance the acquisition by the Ministry of the Interior of trucks and the cost of winter navigation. Therefore, the real reserve, which would remain so-called unforeseen costs, is significantly lower than the amount reported in the state budget on the reserve line. It was also envisaged as a source of cover that a reduction in the rate of income tax could be avoided. It is known that the income tax rate will fall from 21% to 20% in 2015. The decision itself was taken a long time ago, in 2011, but will enter into force next year. One of the reasons why this exclusion was critical is that in reality less than two months have elapsed since the rate of income tax was reduced, so that the time perspective would probably not be very reasonable in order to bring tax back to 21%. Although, what we are denying here in the Commission, we were given historical examples when tax rates have been raised even faster, actually in the middle of the financial year. Now the Commission's procedural decisions and proposals. The Committee on Finance's proposal is to conclude the second reading of the draft (with 6 Commission members in favour, 1 against and no abstentions). The Committee on Monetary Affairs proposed that the deadline for tabling amendments be set at 5.15 p.m. on 27 November. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U157	Thank you. Dear waitress, Between the first and second readings, there was a very fundamental new information. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U158	Thank you. In fact, this did not come as a surprise to those members of the Commission who had examined the explanatory statement on the state budget. Since this topic has been up, I have found the right page during the question. On page 15 of the explanatory memorandum to the state budget, this issue is well reflected. So this topic is not new in Estonia, it is not new even in the sense that it is not the subject of this year. The history of this theme dates back to 2009 and 2010, i.e. to the so-called fluctuating years. The reason is that the Commission assesses our GDP gap differently, which, in a simplified way, means the difference between potential GDP and real GDP, and which is an important component in the calculation of the structural balance. As far as Estonia is concerned, I am simply saying that it is adjusted and the coefficient for which this gap is levelled is 0.44. That is one important reason. If we go a little deeper into this issue, the difference lies first and foremost in our assessment of the situation on the labour market. In simple terms, the European Commission is saying that our current employment, including unemployment, is so-called in line with our potential GDP. This is mainly because wage increases have been relatively rapid in Estonia in recent years. Our argument, in fact, the statement of the Ministry of Finance, and the statement of Eesti Pank, and the IMF and the OECD, also agree with us, is that it is not correct to look at the matter in such a simplified way, because our wage growth is being driven by the external environment. Above all, this is the reason for our northern neighbours. Here comes the 'economic calculation gap', which concerns GDP and, consequently, the exact cycle in which we are in. But the subject itself is old. According to the assessment of the Ministry of Finance, the budget is in a structural surplus of 0.8%, as estimated by the European Commission. On the basis of the European Commission's assessment, the deficit was even higher in 2013. I am now going to say a number that I think was. 1. Well, that's a question of interpretation, isn't it? The European Commission has not approached us in any way to correct its position. The move is also taking place, in our sense, towards increasing the surplus, but in the context of the European Commission, towards reducing the deficit. So there's no real reason for any news and great drama in itself. In addition, I note that the gap, in addition to this GDP gap, also concerns one-off costs. Indeed, as we know, we compensate for the payments made to the pension pillar to those who continued to pay even when the state suspended their contributions to the second pillar. Interestingly, when we stopped these payments, it was considered a 'one time, last time' measure, which was not taken into account, but now that we have restored this payment, it is no longer considered a one-off measure. Once again, it was previously considered a one-off relief and it did not go into the calculation of the structural balance, but it is now. Here we also have a clear point of debate with the European Commission. These issues can be discussed and debated with the Commission, but there is no indication that we should make corrections. I think that 300 million euros can theoretically be calculated there, but the underlying reason is somewhere else.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U222	Thank you, Madam President. Honourable Chairman of the Commission! Next year, the State plans to increase dividend income by 11.6%, i.e. a total of EUR 58,24 million. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U223	Yeah, I'm sure it's manageable. I mean, a dividend that doesn't exist can't be taken out or, actually, what doesn't happen can't be taken out. The main difference is due to the EUR 20 million to be taken from AS Elering. It is generally the case that, in order not to become a so-called "superdividend', a dividend is regarded as a one-off income, it must not exceed the average dividend for the previous three years. In addition, there is a limit related to the volume of GDP. If this is done, the budgetary balance will no longer be improved, but will become a 'one time, last time' transaction and get out of balance. At present, the dividend distribution plan for state-owned enterprises is fully in line with these principles and there are no problems. The increase is not very marked. If we recall 2013 for example, almost EUR 130 million or at least this size of dividends were withdrawn. It was essentially a little over two times more than planned next year. It has been agreed between euro area countries to introduce additional requirements for countries under the structural balance requirement that apply to nominal fiscal positions. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U250	Thank you. The subject was not raised directly from this angle, but it certainly ran through discussions in ministries and in different areas. I'm trying to generalize it somehow. There are certainly some areas where problems can be seen in the future. These are not new problems; they have been through discussions. Above all, there has been talk of costs relating, on the one hand, to revenue, but in one form or another, also fixed by law. Some of the causes are relatively inevitable, in particular demographic developments and, in the long term, the pressure on both pensions and health care. Every government, when preparing a budget or budgetary strategy in the short term, must look at it in the light of a slightly longer or medium-term perspective. There's definitely some debate about these things, actually, it's been going on for a long time. The fact that we are born little and we live longer will inevitably require certain decisions. Medication gets more expensive, but also better, life expectancy increases. It is remembered that retirement age once tended to be at the same level as life expectancy. Then, when the average life expectancy increased, the retirement age came down. Now there is a situation where pension age is adjusted as one measure in many countries to ensure the sustainability of pensions. I think it's unfortunately or luckily inevitable. However, as far as the budgetary context is concerned, also in terms of structural balance, it has been discussed that even the second pillar of the pension is caused by the demographic situation. The pension of this generation, represented by some in this Chamber, including I, who stand here, is unlikely to be covered by the future company in the labour market. So there are many of these measures, one is a retirement pension and the other is a change in the retirement age. However, promoting the participation of pensioners in employment is also one of the measures used in different countries. I think that this should be encouraged more in Estonia.

If you look at the local government's own budgets, there is the largest share of education expenditure and next tends to come, depending on the municipality, all kinds of cultural and sports funding costs. Certainly, municipalities have an important role to play in financing sport, but there is no doubt that the state also has a wide range of financing measures. Next year, the biggest change in budgetary terms is the support for young trainers, which has caused even the most talk this year. It is not a very big secret that so far many youth coaches have not been paid for their work under the Employment Contracts Act, but in other ways. The budget has been allocated to compensate for the change. For example, a number of NGOs, as well as municipalities, where sports schools exist in my home country, are additional revenues that are received in the budget, because wages or remuneration paid for work have been paid in the form of salaries under the Employment Contracts Act in the past, so this additional amount is an additional bonus. I think this is the biggest change in the budget in the area of sport and culture compared to this year's budget.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U322	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Thank you for the opportunity I have been given to speak on this floor. When it comes to next year's budget, I would first like to express my frustration at the structure of the budget. I cannot agree in any way with the former Minister Jürgen Ligi's view that the budget is not belletristika and it must be incomprehensible. The structure of the budget must be simple and logical so that every citizen can understand it. The Auditor General has also indicated that a year after year the state budget is made so incomprehensible that it is not possible to understand what the money is spending on. At the time I was studying at the university, I was interested in materials relating to the state budget and I have to admit that they were very well drafted, everything was understandable and clear. The budget for next year on our table today is difficult to understand. And more importantly, there's no way to find anything at all. Every place of interest needs to be searched for for a long time. But actually, I wanted to talk more about other things. In the case of a country, we very often talk about two types of governance: political governance and state-manly governance. The country can also be run as a politician who is based solely on short-term goals and populism. However, statesmanly behaviour is based on long-term goals. If we look at the budget for next year, we will find a number of signs that point to political governance. Lowering the income tax rate is a political objective, not a state-manly step. In the name of populism, revenue is abandoned, which is easy to give up, but which is difficult to recover later. The consequence is that the country is getting thinner, not to mention that the wealthier win more than the poorer population from reducing the tax rate. Let me give you an example here. If a person receives a median salary of EUR 753 per month, he or she will win more than EUR 5 from the reduction in the income tax rate. If a person earns 3000 euros, he or she wins more than 27 euros. The difference is multiple. Speaking of 70 million lost due to a reduction in income tax rates, we are talking about unpaid subsidies, unbuilt roads and dying rural areas. With this same budget, we will restore funeral support, but only people in need of social assistance will receive it. We offer pensioners dental care, but only for EUR 19. I ask what dental care can be offered for 19 euros. That's ridiculous! Not to mention the lack of employment programmes and greater economic stimulus, which cannot be mentioned in this budget. Or we could take on an increase in the responsibilities of the local governments. Government representatives report indiscriminately which responsibilities must be assumed by the municipalities, while money is not or will not be provided to fulfil these obligations. All development will remain behind populism. The tax is easy to lower, but we should think about the state as a whole. The country must not become very thin. If we lower income tax, what happens next? Will we see, as the next step, after 1 March, that the country is again forced to raise VAT? In this Chamber, one of my colleagues asked what are the priorities of this budget and what are the objectives of the budget. In the light of this budget, can we say what we are doing to boost growth? Is the budget talking about how the economy can work better? The former Minister of Finance did not even bother to answer that question. But I suspect the government doesn't even know what our priorities are. Year after year, a large proportion of the budget has been made up of funds from European Union funds. Already, however, we have to ask what happens when we no longer have the European Union's money. At present, life revolves around when European Union funds are reopened. What if the money is no longer available? No one's talking about it. There will be preparations for the next elections, but in the long term, they will not think. Nobody talks about targets, they talk about politics, but the budget is a real policy-influence mechanism. I believe that this is primarily a pre-election budget. We see purely short-term goals and populism. What about state-manly thinking? Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-19_U330	Ladies and gentlemen, It is a pity that there are considerably fewer of you in this Chamber than there are observers on the Internet of the sitting, but I understand that there are much more important issues to discuss in the budgetary procedure. On the one hand, the government has to be commended for the fact that there are no major stupidities in the budget, but there are also no decisions that are important for Estonia. The result is a slight lurr. In large terms, the main problem with the budget is the absence of a major narrative, based on the words of Prime Minister Taavi Rõivas. In this context, I am particularly concerned about the European Commission's recent assessment of the structural government deficit of 0.7% of GDP in the next two years. Please note that we are now talking not only about nominal deficits, but also about structural deficits! The differences between the European Union and Estonia's forecasts are due both to the different assessment of the GDP gap and to which measures are taken into account as one-off factors. On the other hand, page 15 of the explanatory memorandum to the 2015 draft state budget states that the assessment of budgetary policy and recommendations will be based on the European Commission's forecasts. We, too, with the new basic law on the state budget adopted in spring, stipulated that the state budget must be structurally balanced or in surplus. As we read yesterday's overview of the state budget of the Legal and Analytical Department of the Riigikogu, experts have also reached a worrying conclusion. According to the calculations of the Fiscal Board at Eesti Pank, there is a high risk in 2014 and 2015 that the Estonian government has estimated the structural budget surplus to be higher than the actual one. That's polite. I think everyone can read between the lines. Ladies and gentlemen, Today we're playing some weird game whose rules can be bent, interpreted and changed. These are clear signs of danger. For this reason, the IRL Group proposed, in the procedure of the draft state budget, a reduction in government expenditure of EUR 10 million in order to improve the balance of the state budget and make it more effective. We have already promised at first reading that we will not ignore the strengthening of the Estonian state border. The government has allocated EUR 0.9 million to the national border in the draft budget, which is clearly too little in view of the current security situation. According to the IRL amendment, an additional EUR 4 million would be earmarked for strengthening the border. The Estonian border with Russia is the external border of the European Union, and its development should be one of our country's priorities in the coming years, and it needs to move quickly towards it. This is not just about the case of the Eston Suitcase, but, above all, about whether our eastern border is holding. I am thinking of preventing both cross-border smuggling and trafficking in human beings. Our biggest cost amendment is to allocate EUR 15 million to farmers for national transitional support. If you look at the budget by sector, some of the biggest losers will be farmers next year, whose support was simply sharply shut down. The competitiveness of farmers in an uncertain market situation due to Russia would be a strong blow if they were to be deprived of the subsidies that are also paid in our neighbouring countries. In recent weeks, we have talked to some coalition politicians in the corridors of the Riigikogu and asked why farmers have been put in this position. The answer is, well, you get it, the Reform Party wants it. I honestly don't understand! The portfolio of the Minister of Agriculture is in the hands of the sots and please defend your position not only in words but also in deeds! We also proposed to invest nearly EUR 2 million in R &amp; D. If we want to ensure the stable development of research institutions in scientific disciplines relevant to Estonia's socio-economic development, this is the least that should be added. In addition, the IRL proposed to allocate a resource for the preparation and implementation of the laws necessary for the creation of a single parent maintenance fund. In Estonia, one in four children grows in a single parent's family, and in many cases they do not receive the allowance that a separate parent should pay. Such children are among the most economically difficult members of society, so their development opportunities are limited and make up a significant proportion of children living below the poverty line. The draft decision of the Riigikogu has also been submitted by the IRL to Parliament's proceedings. We also want to intensify the implementation of the administrative reform. The inevitability of administrative reform was lastly highlighted by the Auditor General of the National Audit Office during the presentation of the annual report. Most parties support it, employers' organisations support it, society supports it, but the Reform Party does not support it. And that has been the case for years. Honestly, it's embarrassing. We could have considerably fewer municipalities, significantly fewer public earners and much stronger local attraction centres. According to our proposal, money is intended for the preparation and implementation of the laws necessary for administrative reform. In summary, the biggest problem is that Estonia stands still in its development. The perception of the state's stagnation is general. It was just referred to by the Auditor General; it is felt in the private sector; we can also see this in comparison with the southern neighbours of Latvia and Lithuania. At our current level of development, an economic growth of 1 to 2% is not the way we catch up with neighbouring countries on the other side of the Baltic Sea. Estonia must have its own story and its own purpose. A simple redistribution of money and a continuous increase in subsidies cannot be a success story for Estonia. Nobody else will eat the soup we're cooking for us today, even if the soup is lean. In order to render it mute, we propose that the second reading of the draft state budget be interrupted. Let us take a moment to avoid losing another important year to Estonia! Thank you.
